People text me about class or ask if I’m coming, and I just ignore it because even answering feels like too much. None of it feels worth the effort right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive since you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… um, that’s actually what scared me enough to finally say something at my prenatal appointment. I wasn’t going in planning to talk about all this—I was there for the usual stuff, and they had those little forms, and I kept thinking about how I’ve handed forms like that to patients before and told them, “Just answer honestly,” like it’s easy.\n\nBut… yes. I’ve had thoughts like I wish I just wouldn’t wake up. Not like I have a plan to hurt myself, I don’t. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or not having to be here feeling like this. It scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns since you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: My appetite, yeah… it’s been off. I know I should be eating regularly, especially being pregnant, and I feel guilty about that because I’m a nurse, I’m always telling people not to skip meals or just run on coffee, you know?\n\nBut most days food just sounds… unappealing. I’ll get halfway through a clinic shift and realize I’ve only had crackers, or coffee, and then I’m like, okay, that’s not good. But even then it’s hard to make myself eat anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slower, definitely. It’s like everything I do is through syrup or something. I’ll be at the clinic and I know exactly what I need—like gathering wound supplies, or finishing charting—and it just takes me so much longer than it should.\n\nAnd I hate that, because normally I’m pretty efficient. I’m the person who’s like, okay, let’s get the room turned over, let’s get the next thing done. I used to joke with one of the MAs that I could stock a room half-asleep, which… I guess isn’t even funny right now. But lately I’ll just stand there for a second staring at the cabinet, like my body hasn’t caught up with what my brain is asking it to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like work or reading since you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… concentration has been bad. I keep rereading medication orders or wound-care notes because my brain just feels foggy, like the words don’t stick the first time.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because those are things I’m usually careful with anyway, but normally I can process them and move on. Lately I’ll look at something I know, something routine, and I freeze. Like, should I double-check that again? Did I miss something? And then I’m second-guessing decisions that used to feel automatic.\n\nIt makes the whole day feel harder, because I’m not… I’m not trusting my own brain the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of heavy sadness are interfering with your ability to function at work or at home most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up to work. I don’t want it to sound like I’m just not functioning at all, because I am there, I’m doing my shifts, but… it’s not the same.\n\nMy supervisor actually mentioned that some of my charting has been incomplete, which felt awful. I’ve always been the one who double-checks things, you know? And at home, or outside of work, I’ve kind of been disappearing from people. Friends will text and I’ll just… not answer, or I’ll tell myself I’ll respond later and then I don’t.\n\nI used to have little things that kept me steady, like seeing friends or doing normal routines, and lately I’ve been pulling away from all of that. So, yeah… it is interfering. I’m still getting through the day, but not well.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these feelings of heavy sadness are present every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. It’s been like that for the past few weeks, this heavy sad pressure just sitting on me most of the day.\n\nEven at work, I’ll be smiling at patients and saying the normal things—because you kind of have to, you know, people are already scared or in pain and I don’t want to add to that—but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s not like it just comes and goes for a few minutes. It’s… there. Most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these feelings of heavy sadness are severe enough that they're nearly unbearable, or are they more of a constant, manageable discomfort?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s unbearable every second, like I’m going to collapse right there, but… it’s heavy. It’s this constant sad pressure that’s been sitting on me almost all day for the past few weeks.\n\nLike at work, I can still smile at patients and do the polite nurse voice, you know, because you have to keep things calm for them—but inside I feel like I’m barely keeping myself together. So… manageable maybe from the outside, but it doesn’t feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any times of the day where you feel slightly better, or is the sadness consistently the same intensity throughout the day?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly the same, I think. I mean, I came in because for the past few weeks it’s been this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me almost all day.\n\nSometimes I’m distracted for a minute at work, like if I’m talking to a patient and doing the nurse-smile thing, but it doesn’t really mean I feel better. It’s more like I’m covering it, if that makes sense. Inside I still feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really, no. I used to have things after work that kind of helped me feel like a person again. Like I’d paint sometimes, or go to yoga, and I was doing salsa for a while—my friend kept dragging me there and it was actually fun once I got over feeling awkward.\n\nBut lately after my shifts I just go home and sit on the couch. People text me about class or meeting up, and I just… don’t answer. It all feels like too much effort, even the stuff I used to look forward to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any unusual thoughts or heard any voices that others couldn't hear since you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: Um… voices? No, I don’t think so. I mean, sometimes I get so in my head that I’ll replay conversations from work, like if I said the wrong thing to a patient or forgot to chart something, and then I’ll sit there thinking about it way too long. But that’s just me thinking, not like… hearing someone.\n\nAnd unusual thoughts, I’m not totally sure what counts as unusual. I haven’t had anything like seeing things or hearing things other people don’t hear. It’s more just… everything feels heavy and I’m tired of feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that there are any days where you feel even slightly happy or relieved, or is the sadness always present?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s pretty much always there. I mean, I can laugh for a second if someone at work says something ridiculous, because patients and coworkers do that sometimes, and you kind of have to keep moving, you know?\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like real relief. For the past few weeks it’s been this heavy, sad pressure almost all day. Even when I’m smiling at patients and acting normal, inside I still feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any days where you don't feel overwhelmed by this sadness, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think if there’s been a day where it lifted, but it’s more like it’s just sitting there on me almost all day. I came in because it’s been a few weeks of that heavy, sad pressure, and even at work—like, I can be smiling at patients, doing what I’m supposed to do, making small talk about their grandkids or whatever—but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSometimes I’ll get distracted for a minute if things are busy, but it doesn’t feel like it’s gone. It’s just… pushed to the side for a second, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I mean… I used to. After my shifts I’d usually paint a little, or I’d go to yoga, or sometimes salsa class if I could drag myself there. It was kind of how I’d shake off the day, especially after a rough shift.\n\nLately, though, I just go home and sit on the couch. I’ll see texts come in and just… not answer them. Even stuff I used to like feels like too much effort, like I can’t make myself care enough to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience any pleasure when good things happen, or has that been absent lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels pretty absent lately. I’ll get halfway through a clinic shift and realize I’ve only had crackers, or coffee, and then I’m like, okay, that’s not good. But even then it’s hard to make myself eat anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slower, definitely. It’s like everything I do is through syrup or something. I’ll be at the clinic and I know exactly what I need—like gathering wound supplies, or finishing charting—and it just takes me so much longer than it should.\n\nAnd I hate that, because normally I’m pretty efficient. I’m the person who’s like, okay, let’s get the room turned over, let’s get the next thing done. I used to joke with one of the MAs that I could stock a room half-asleep, which… I guess isn’t even funny right now. But lately I’ll just stand there for a second staring at the cabinet, like my body hasn’t caught up with what my brain is asking it to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like work or reading since you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… concentration has been bad. I keep rereading medication orders or wound-care notes because my brain just feels foggy, like the words don’t stick the first time.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because those are things I’m usually careful with anyway, but normally I can process them and move on. Lately I’ll look at something I know, something routine, and I freeze. Like, should I double-check that again? Did I miss something? And then I’m second-guessing decisions that used to feel automatic.\n\nIt makes the whole day feel harder, because I’m not… I’m not trusting my own brain the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of heavy sadness are interfering with your ability to function at work or at home most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up to work. I don’t want it to sound like I’m just not functioning at all, because I am there, I’m doing my shifts, but… it’s not the same.\n\nMy supervisor actually mentioned that some of my charting has been incomplete, which felt awful. I’ve always been the one who double-checks things, you know? And at home, or outside of work, I’ve kind of been disappearing from people. Friends will text and I’ll just… not answer, or I’ll tell myself I’ll respond later and then I don’t.\n\nI used to have little things that kept me steady, like seeing friends or doing normal routines, and lately I’ve been pulling away from all of that. So, yeah… it is interfering. I’m still getting through the day, but not well.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these feelings of heavy sadness are present every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. It’s been like that for the past few weeks, this heavy sad pressure just sitting on me most of the day.\n\nEven at work, I’ll be smiling at patients and saying the normal things—because you kind of have to, you know, people are already scared or in pain and I don’t want to add to that—but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s not like it just comes and goes for a few minutes. It’s… there. Most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these feelings of heavy sadness are severe enough that they're nearly unbearable, or are they more of a constant, manageable discomfort?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s unbearable every second, like I’m going to collapse right there, but… it’s heavy. It’s this constant sad pressure that’s been sitting on me almost all day for the past few weeks.\n\nLike at work, I can still smile at patients and do the polite nurse voice, you know, because you have to keep things calm for them—but inside I feel like I’m barely keeping myself together. So… manageable maybe from the outside, but it doesn’t feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any times of the day where you feel slightly better, or is the sadness consistently the same intensity throughout the day?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly the same, I think. I mean, I came in because for the past few weeks it’s been this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me almost all day.\n\nSometimes I’m distracted for a minute at work, like if I’m talking to a patient and doing the nurse-smile thing, but it doesn’t really mean I feel better. It’s more like I’m covering it, if that makes sense. Inside I still feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or enjoyment in?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really, no. I used to have things after work that kind of helped me feel like a person again. Like I’d paint sometimes, or go to yoga, and I was doing salsa for a while—my friend kept dragging me there and it was actually fun once I got over feeling awkward.\n\nBut lately after my shifts I just go home and sit on the couch. People text me about class or meeting up, and I just… don’t answer. It all feels like too much effort, even the stuff I used to look forward to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any unusual thoughts or heard any voices that others couldn't hear since you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: Um… voices? No, I don’t think so. I mean, sometimes I get so in my head that I’ll replay conversations from work, like if I said the wrong thing to a patient or forgot to chart something, and then I’ll sit there thinking about it way too long. But that’s just me thinking, not like… hearing someone.\n\nAnd unusual thoughts, I’m not totally sure what counts as unusual. I haven’t had anything like seeing things or hearing things other people don’t hear. It’s more just… everything feels heavy and I’m tired of feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that there are any days where you feel even slightly happy or relieved, or is the sadness always present?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s pretty much always there. I mean, I can laugh for a second if someone at work says something ridiculous, because patients and coworkers do that sometimes, and you kind of have to keep moving, you know?\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like real relief. For the past few weeks it’s been this heavy, sad pressure almost all day. Even when I’m smiling at patients and acting normal, inside I still feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any days where you don't feel overwhelmed by this sadness, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think if there’s been a day where it lifted, but it’s more like it’s just sitting there on me almost all day. I came in because it’s been a few weeks of that heavy, sad pressure, and even at work—like, I can be smiling at patients, doing what I’m supposed to do, making small talk about their grandkids or whatever—but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSometimes I’ll get distracted for a minute if things are busy, but it doesn’t feel like it’s gone. It’s just… pushed to the side for a second, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I mean… I used to. After my shifts I’d usually paint a little, or I’d go to yoga, or sometimes salsa class if I could drag myself there. It was kind of how I’d shake off the day, especially after a rough shift.\n\nLately, though, I just go home and sit on the couch. I’ll see texts come in and just… not answer them. Even stuff I used to like feels like too much effort, like I can’t make myself care enough to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience any pleasure when good things happen, or has that been absent lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels pretty absent lately. Like, I know when something is supposed to feel good, but it doesn’t really land.\n\nAfter my shifts, I used to paint or go to yoga, sometimes salsa if I had the energy—there was this one instructor who was always way too cheerful, but somehow it helped. Now I just go home and sit on the couch. My phone will buzz with texts and I’ll look at it and just… leave it there. It all feels like too much effort, and not really worth it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any hallucinations or delusions since you started feeling this heavy sadness?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve been so tired and in my own head that sometimes it feels like everything around me is kind of far away, but not like I’m seeing or hearing things that aren’t there.\n\nAnd delusions… I’m not totally sure what counts as that. At work we talk about stuff like that sometimes, but when it’s about me I kind of blank. I don’t think I’ve had anything like that, though. Just the sadness feeling really heavy.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your heavy sadness is worse in the morning or does it stay about the same throughout the day?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of there most of the day, honestly. I don’t know if mornings are worse exactly. Like, I wake up and it’s already this heavy sad pressure sitting on me, and then I go to work and I can smile at patients and say the right things, because you have to, you know? People are scared or in pain and you can’t just fall apart in front of them.\n\nBut inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. And then it just sort of follows me through the day. Maybe I notice it more when things get quiet, but it doesn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your heavy sadness is significantly interfering with your daily life, such as causing problems at work or with relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is, I think. I mean, I’m still showing up for work, so part of me keeps wanting to say it’s not that bad, but… my supervisor did mention my charting. Like, not in a harsh way, just that some of it’s been incomplete, and that’s not really like me.\n\nAnd with friends, I’ve kind of just… disappeared. I keep meaning to answer people, and then I don’t. I used to have things that kept me steady—painting, yoga, just meeting someone for coffee—and now I keep backing out or not making plans at all. It’s weird because I know those things help, or they used to, but I can’t seem to get myself there."
